Here, the matter trace is defined by contracting the energy-momentum tensor with the metric from the original matter action. In the absence of explicit lambda-corrections, the theory inherits the vacuum logarithmic-connection hairy branch. To obtain a non-zero trace, we introduce polynomial non-linear electrodynamics and construct an exact magnetic branch in coincident Cartesian Kerr-Schild coordinates. The non-metricity scalar of this branch vanishes, while its matter trace is proportional to the inverse eighth power of the distance. Its metric component function is given by 1 - 2M/r + q^2/r^2 + C/r^6, where the correction term C equals xi(1 - 4 lambda)q^4 / 10. Consequently, the quadratic non-metricity coupling is sterile on this branch. We derive the metric and matter equations, the Euclidean temperature, the single-horizon remnant, the master scalar equation, and the first-order gauge shift. The logarithmic derivative of the matter trace imparts a winding number of -8 on a closed contour enclosing the central singularity, but a winding number of zero on a sufficiently small horizon contour. Nevertheless, the temperature and scalar propagation still vary continuously with the correction coefficient. Therefore, the trace winding number classifies divisor data rather than superseding the matter trace or determining its backreaction. This analysis also demonstrates a degeneracy between the trace coupling coefficient and the non-linear electromagnetic coefficient. These results are specific to this particular branch; this paper does not expound upon the full coupled gravity spectrum, nor does it claim priority for this magnetic metric.

</details>


### [95] [Robustness-based selection of laser-locking architectures for time-delay interferometry in space-based gravitational-wave detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39432)
*Hao-Kang Chen,Pan-Pan Wang,Cheng-Gang Shao*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper introduces a quantitative framework to evaluate the robustness of phase-reference architectures in space-based gravitational-wave detectors, using directed graphs and percolation theory; it shows that disturbance location, reference dependence, and support extent critically influence optimal architecture selection—beyond traditional criteria like reference depth or local locking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the vulnerability of phase-reference architectures to disturbances in space-based gravitational-wave detectors, where a single reference failure can degrade laser phase locking and time-delay interferometry (TDI), necessitating a robustness-aware design criterion.

Method: Uses cascade equations and percolation-theoretic measures on directed reference graphs with Erdős–Rényi support layers; evaluates robustness under uniform and nonuniform disturbances, incorporates partial dependence and connected-support criteria; compares configurations via reference-depth distribution, local-lock baseline, and optical-input support requirements for TDI.

Result: Robustness ranking depends on disturbance profile: uniform disturbances favor shallow reference-depth distributions; nonuniform disturbances and partial dependence shift preference toward configurations with greater downstream connected support and limited degradation accumulation; requiring connected support on *all* spacecraft (vs. ≥1) yields different TDI-optimal architectures.

Conclusion: Robustness—quantified via graph-theoretic and statistical models—is a decisive, complementary criterion to phase-locking feasibility and interferometric performance; architecture selection must jointly consider disturbance location, reference dependence structure, and required support extent.

Abstract: Space-based gravitational-wave detectors compare laser phases exchanged between distant spacecraft. Offset phase locking keeps beatnotes in the phasemeter band, while time-delay interferometry suppresses laser frequency noise. A disturbance of one reference can degrade the functions that depend on it, making robustness an additional criterion for choosing a phase-reference architecture. We introduce a quantitative comparison based on established cascade equations and percolation measures, with directed reference graphs and Erdős--Rényi support layers. Under uniform disturbances, the robustness score is determined by the reference-depth distribution. Under nonuniform disturbances and partial dependence, the connected-support criterion can select a different configuration from both the published depth and local-lock criteria and a reference-path baseline. The phase-locking comparisons favor configurations that limit accumulated degradation and supply downstream lasers from references retaining greater connected support. Paired finite-network calculations support these differences in preference. In a separate comparison of optical-input support for time-delay interferometry, requiring connected support on all spacecraft gives a different preference from requiring it on at least one spacecraft. These findings identify disturbance location, reference dependence and the required extent of support as factors that can change architecture selection. Within the stated statistical model, robustness rankings can complement phase-locking feasibility and interferometric performance when prioritizing configurations for space-based gravitational-wave detectors.

</details>


### [96] [Covariant phase space analysis of static spherical vacua in power-law $f(R)$ gravity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39484)
*Mariam Campbell,Eduardo Bittencourt,Peter K. S. Dunsby*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper analyzes static, spherically symmetric vacuum solutions in power-law f(R) gravity using a (1+1+2) covariant autonomous system; it identifies critical points (Clifton–Barrow solution, Schwarzschild endpoint, Minkowski), studies their stability via Poincaré compactification and extended charts, and reconstructs notable geometries including a scalar-flat throat solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically classify and understand the structure and stability of static, spherically symmetric vacuum solutions in power-law f(R) gravity — a key class of modified gravity theories — beyond standard GR, particularly where exact solutions are scarce and qualitative dynamics are obscured by coordinate or parametrization artifacts.

Method: Formulates the vacuum field equations as a covariant autonomous dynamical system using the (1+1+2) formalism; applies Poincaré compactification with secondary and weighted charts to resolve asymptotic regimes; performs linear stability analysis of finite and infinite critical points; carries out metric reconstruction from phase-space trajectories.

Result: Identifies three finite critical sets: Clifton–Barrow (scale-invariant) solution (radially outward attractor for a range of power-law exponents), Schwarzschild endpoint (saddle point in polynomially extended system), and degenerate boundaries; discovers normally attracting sheets at large normalized curvature in positive-curvature sector; reconstructs a scalar-flat solution with regular throat, one asymptotically flat end, and one curvature-singular end.

Conclusion: The phase-space analysis organizes known and new vacuum solutions in power-law f(R) gravity, clarifying their global dynamical relationships and stability under static radial evolution; however, it does not yield a full classification of all static spherical vacua, and stability assertions are restricted to the static radial flow.

Abstract: We formulate the static, spherically symmetric vacuum equations of power-law metric $f(R)$ gravity as a covariant autonomous system using the $(1+1+2)$ formalism. The finite critical sets include the scale-invariant Clifton--Barrow solution, a Minkowski endpoint shared by the Schwarzschild family, and degenerate boundaries of the normalized chart. The Clifton--Barrow equilibrium is an outward-radial attractor over a positive interval of the power-law parameter, whereas the Schwarzschild endpoint is a saddle of the polynomially extended system. Poincaré compactification, supplemented by secondary and weighted charts, resolves several asymptotic regimes. In particular, normally attracting sheets at large normalized curvature occur in the positive-curvature sector and are separated from the negative-curvature Clifton--Barrow equilibrium. Metric reconstruction also identifies a selected scalar-flat family with a regular throat, one asymptotically flat end, and a curvature-singular end. These results organize the regular reduced chart and the boundary regimes resolved here; they do not constitute a complete classification of static spherical vacua. All stability statements concern the static radial flow.

</details>


### [97] [Quasinormal Modes of Slowly Rotating Black Holes in Hernquist Halos: Redshift and Angular Momentum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39548)
*Xing-Hui Feng,Bo Wang*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper studies axial quasinormal modes of rotating black holes embedded in Hernquist dark matter halos, using a static analytic spacetime with an inner vacuum cavity and first-order frame-dragging corrections. It quantifies how halo compactness and mass ratio affect mode frequencies, showing redshift-dominated behavior at low compactness and angular-momentum-partition as the key driver of configuration dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how dark matter halos influence black hole quasinormal modes—particularly how halo structure (compactness, mass ratio, angular momentum distribution) modifies axial QNMs beyond Kerr predictions, with implications for gravitational-wave asteroseismology and dark matter environment inference.

Method: Uses an established analytic static geometry for a black hole in a Hernquist halo featuring an inner vacuum cavity extending to 4M_BH; incorporates frame dragging to first order in rotation; computes fundamental and first-overtone axial QNMs for l=2; varies mass ratio ε = M_BH/M_halo (0.1, 0.01) and halo compactness C = M_halo/a_0 (10⁻⁴–0.3); applies local Kerr approximation with redshift rescaling, horizon angular momentum–driven rotational splitting, and uniform frame-dragging–induced real shift.

Result: At zero spin, cavity redshift reproduces Kerr QNM frequencies with <0.04% (fundamental) and <0.31% (first overtone) relative residuals; weak-compactness limit (1−C) explains insensitivity to ε in dilute halos; exact redshift accounts for most separation between ε=0.1 and ε=0.01 sequences at higher C; real spin coefficient trends invert depending on whether local angular momentum or coordinate angular velocity vanishes; at C=0.3, real fundamental-mode spin coefficients differ by ~38% and ~54% across configurations, but fixing horizon angular momentum reduces differences to <1% for both overtones.

Conclusion: Axial QNMs of black holes in Hernquist halos are dominantly governed by cavity-induced gravitational redshift and horizon angular momentum—not global halo angular velocity—making horizon J_H a more robust physical parameter than coordinate or local angular momentum definitions; this clarifies the role of dark matter environment in QNM spectroscopy and supports using QNMs to probe halo compactness and angular momentum partition.

Abstract: We investigate axial quasinormal modes of black holes embedded in Hernquist halos, using an established analytic static geometry with an inner vacuum cavity extending to $4M_{\mathrm{BH}}$ and solving for frame dragging to first order in rotation. We compute the fundamental and first overtone branches for mass ratios $\epsilon\equiv M_{\mathrm{BH}}/M_{\mathrm{halo}}$ of $0.1$ and $0.01$, and halo compactness $\mathcal C\equiv M_{\mathrm{halo}}/a_0$ in the range $10^{-4}\leq\mathcal C\leq0.3$. The cavity geometry gives a local Kerr estimate in which redshift rescales the vacuum frequencies, horizon angular momentum sets the rotational splitting, and uniform frame dragging adds a real frequency shift. At zero spin, the cavity redshift reproduces the complex $\ell=2$ frequencies with relative residuals below $0.04\%$ for the fundamental and $0.31\%$ for the first overtone. Its leading weak compactness behavior, $1-\mathcal C$, explains the weak mass ratio dependence in dilute halos; at larger compactness, the exact redshift accounts for most of the separation between the two mass ratio sequences. At fixed total angular momentum, halos with vanishing local angular momentum and those with vanishing coordinate angular velocity exhibit opposite compactness trends in the real spin coefficient. At $\mathcal C=0.3$, their real fundamental mode spin coefficients differ by approximately $38\%$ and $54\%$ for the two mass ratios. Fixing the horizon angular momentum reduces these differences below $1\%$ for both overtones, showing that angular momentum partition accounts for most of the configuration dependence.

</details>


### [98] [Quasinormal Mode Excitation in the Horizon Shear of Near-Extremal Kerr Black Holes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39729)
*Brennen Black*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper identifies low-amplitude, high-frequency 'wiggles' in the horizon shear of near-extremal Kerr black holes perturbed by eccentric extreme mass-ratio inspirals (EMRIs) as damped Kerr quasinormal modes (QNMs), specifically the fundamental (2,1) and (3,2) modes, with supporting evidence for (3,1), (4,1), and (4,2); it further argues these wiggles originate from QNM excitation in the driving tidal field, excluding m=0 and m=ℓ multipoles for ℓ≤4.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To resolve the origin of unexplained high-frequency 'wiggles' in horizon shear after periapsis passage in near-extremal Kerr black holes perturbed by eccentric EMRIs — previously misattributed as non-QNM phenomena due to lack of orbital frequency correspondence.

Method: Time-domain fitting of horizon shear waveforms from numerical simulations of equatorial prograde EMRIs (p=3M, e=0.85) on Kerr backgrounds with spins a/M ∈ [0.999, 0.99999]; multipole-resolved QNM identification via least-squares fitting against Kerr QNM frequencies and damping times; comparison with tidal field decomposition.

Result: The (2,1) and (3,2) damped QNMs are dominantly excited and fit within 2σ of theoretical Kerr values; (3,1), (4,1), (4,2) are detected in multipole-separated fits but not resolvable in full signal; no QNM excitation found in m=0 or m=ℓ multipoles for ℓ≤4; wiggles are shown to be present in the driving tidal field.

Conclusion: The horizon shear 'wiggles' are genuine Kerr damped QNMs — not artifacts or non-modal features — confirming that even highly dynamical, asymmetric EMRI perturbations can selectively excite specific damped QNM branches in near-extremal regimes, with implications for gravitational-wave modeling and black-hole spectroscopy.

Abstract: The horizon shear of a near-extremal Kerr black hole perturbed by an eccentric extreme mass ratio inspiral shows low amplitude, high frequency oscillations (''wiggles") after periapsis passage. Previous works are unable to tie the frequency of the wiggles to the orbit, and argue they cannot be quasinormal modes (QNMs). We show that they are Kerr QNMs belonging to the damped-mode branch --- that is, modes whose decay rate stays finite as the spin approaches extremality. For an equatorial prograde orbit with $p = 3M$ and $e = 0.85$ and spins ranging from $a/M = 0.999$ to $a/M = 0.99999$, fits to the time-domain shear identify the fundamental $(\ell,m) = (2,1)$ and $(3,2)$ modes as the dominant and first subdominant excitations. Fitting each multipole separately gives frequencies to within two standard deviations of the Kerr values and provides evidence for the excitation of the fundamental $(3,1)$, $(4,1)$ and $(4,2)$ modes, though these are not resolvable in the full signal. We further argue that the same wiggles must be present in the tidal field that drives the shear. For $\ell \leq 4$ we find no evidence of QNM excitation in the $m = 0$ or the $m = \ell$ multipoles.

</details>


### [99] [Topological Interpretations in Anisotropic Kerr-Newman-AdS Black Hole Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39732)
*Sreeshma A,Md Sabir Ali,C.L. Ahmed Rizwan*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper applies Duan's φ-mapping theory and complex residue methods to analyze the thermodynamic topology of anisotropic Kerr-Newman-AdS black holes, revealing how global topological numbers (winding numbers and total topological charge W) classify phase structures — with AdS curvature inducing a topologically nontrivial (W = +1) state, while its absence (Kerr-Newman) yields trivial topology (W = 0), and anisotropy preserves the AdS class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover universal, coordinate-independent features of black hole phase transitions by linking thermodynamic equilibrium states to topological defects in parameter space, leveraging the robustness of topological classification against coordinate choices.

Method: Constructs a generalized off-shell free energy and defines a thermodynamic vector field in parameter space; identifies equilibrium states as zero points (defects) of this field; computes winding numbers via Duan's φ-mapping theory and complex residue techniques; compares global topological number W across limiting cases (anisotropic → isotropic, AdS → flat).

Result: Anisotropic Kerr-Newman-AdS and Kerr-Newman-AdS black holes each possess one defect with winding number +1 ⇒ W = +1; Kerr-Newman (no AdS) has two defects (+1 and −1) ⇒ W = 0; Kiselev-type black hole analysis confirms topological sensitivity to metric reduction conditions; complex residue method consistently reproduces winding numbers.

Conclusion: The presence of AdS curvature fundamentally alters the global thermodynamic topology (nontrivial W), whereas anisotropy does not change the topological class; thermodynamic topology thus serves as a universal diagnostic for distinguishing phase structures across black hole families.

Abstract: The interplay between black hole thermodynamics and topology provides a strong, coordinate-independent framework for discovering the underlying structure of black hole phase transitions as well as their universal features. We explore the thermodynamic topology of the anisotropic Kerr-Newman-AdS black hole using Duan's $\phi$-mapping theory and a complex residue approach. By creating the generalized off-shell free energy, we propose a thermodynamic vector field in the parameter space with zero points corresponding to equilibrium black hole states. The winding numbers associated with these defects are utilized to calculate the black hole's global topological number. We begin with an analysis of the entire anisotropic Kerr-Newman-AdS black hole, followed by two limiting examples. Setting the anisotropic parameter $K = 0$ produces the Kerr-Newman-AdS black hole, while eliminating the AdS contribution produces the Kerr-Newman black hole. The anisotropic Kerr-Newman-AdS and Kerr-Newman-AdS black holes have a single thermodynamic defect with winding number $+1$, which results in $W = +1$. In contrast, the Kerr-Newman black hole has two defects with winding numbers $+1$ and $-1$, which result in a vanishing global topological number, $W = 0$. These findings show that the AdS contribution alters the global topological classification, but the anisotropic matter studied here preserves the topological class of the Kerr-Newman-AdS black hole. As an analogous limiting instance, we further study a Kiselev-type black hole, analyzing the circumstances in which its metric reduces to a charged black-hole geometry and how the thermodynamic topology reflects this reduction. Finally, we use the complex residue approach on the anisotropic Kerr-Newman-AdS black hole.

</details>


### [100] [HWF Analysis of TRN Black Holes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39757)
*Ashfaque Ahmad,Sudhir Kumar Gupta,Abbas Ali*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper extends the horizon wave-function formalism to charged, spinless quantum sources in (D+1)-dimensional spacetime, computing horizon wave functions and black hole formation probabilities for both inner and outer horizons of Tangherlini–Reissner–Nordström black holes; it finds that the inner-to-outer horizon probability ratio deviates strongly from unity away from extremality, and formation probabilities are significantly suppressed in higher dimensions, especially for sub-Planckian masses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize the horizon wave-function (HWF) formalism—previously applied to neutral, uncharged sources—to charged, spherically symmetric quantum sources in arbitrary (D+1)-dimensional spacetimes, enabling quantitative analysis of quantum black hole formation for non-extremal, higher-dimensional charged black holes.

Method: The authors construct horizon wave functions for both inner and outer horizons of the Tangherlini–Reissner–Nordström (TRN) black hole in D+1 dimensions using a quantum mechanical treatment of the source mass and charge; they compute formation probabilities P_BH^± by integrating squared HWFs over horizon radius, and analyze parametric dependencies on dimension D, mass m, charge parameter α, and Planck scale Λ_D.

Result: The ratio P_BH^−/P_BH^+ deviates sharply from 1 as the source moves away from extremality (α < 1); black hole formation probabilities decrease exponentially with increasing D, especially for m ≲ Λ_D — e.g., P_BH^+(D=5)/P_BH^+(D=10) ≈ large enhancement factor for α = 0.5.

Conclusion: Quantum black hole formation is highly sensitive to both charge deviation from extremality and spacetime dimensionality; the HWF framework successfully captures distinct quantum behaviors of inner and outer horizons in higher-dimensional charged geometries, suggesting suppressed observability of quantum black holes in high-D scenarios unless near extremality or super-Planckian mass.

Abstract: We extend the horizon wave-function (HWF) formalism to a spherically symmetric, charged, spinless quantum source in $\mathcal{D} + 1$ dimensional spacetime geometry and construct the HWFs associated with inner- as well as outer-horizons of the Tangherlini--Reissner--Nordström (TRN) black hole. The corresponding black hole formation probabilities, $P_{\rm BH}^{\pm}$, are also calculated and their sensitivities due to various source parameters including charge, mass, and $\mathcal{D}$ are analysed as well. Our analysis reveals that the ratio of inner- to outer-horizon probabilities, $P_{\rm BH}^{-}/P_{\rm BH}^{+}$, changes drastically from being unity as the source departs away from being in the extremal limit defined by $\alpha$ ($\to 1$ for extremal case). Furthermore, black hole formation probabilities are found to be highly suppressed for higher values of $\mathcal{D}$, particularly for the source mass, $m \lesssim \Lambda_{\mathcal{D}}$, e.g., for $\alpha = 0.5$, the ratio, $P_{\rm BH}^{+}(\mathcal{D} = 5)/P_{\rm BH}^{+}(\mathcal{D}=10)$ turn out to be approximately associated with $\mathcal{D}$ spatial-dimensions.

</details>


### [101] [Looking for gravitational waves with orbital dynamics: GUEST mission and the $μ$Hz band of Gravitational Waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39881)
*Diego Blas*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper reviews how gravitationally bound systems serve as resonant detectors for microhertz gravitational waves and ultra-light dark matter, and introduces GUEST—a proposed ESA F-class mission using laser-tracked eccentric Earth-orbiting spheres to probe the unexplored microhertz band.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore the under-investigated microhertz gravitational-wave band—bridging pulsar timing arrays and LISA—which may contain signals from supermassive black hole binaries, early-Universe processes, primordial black holes, or ultra-light dark matter.

Method: Analyzes orbital dynamics of gravitationally bound systems as resonant detectors; proposes GUEST, a space mission concept featuring two dense, retroreflector-covered passive spheres in highly eccentric Earth orbits, monitored via global satellite laser ranging.

Result: Demonstrates theoretical feasibility of using orbital resonances for microhertz GW/ULDM detection; presents GUEST as a technically viable, cost-effective pathfinder mission for this frequency band.

Conclusion: The microhertz band is a compelling frontier for fundamental physics; GUEST offers a novel, ground-truth-calibrated approach to access it, complementing existing and future GW observatories.

Abstract: `Adding colour'' to the gravitational-wave sky by probing unexplored frequency bands has the potential to unveil new and unexpected phenomena in the Universe. The microhertz band, between pulsar timing arrays and LISA, is a particularly attractive target: it should host the extension of the signal recently found by pulsar timing arrays (if it is generated by a family of sources of gravitational waves), and it may harbour signals from the early Universe, primordial black holes or dark matter. In these proceedings, we review how the orbits of gravitationally bound systems act as resonant detectors of microhertz gravitational waves and ultra-light dark matter. We then describe GUEST (Gravitational Universe Exploration with Satellite Tracking), a mission concept recently proposed to the ESA F-class call, consisting of two dense passive spheres covered with retroreflectors, placed in highly eccentric Earth orbits and tracked by a global laser-ranging network.

</details>


### [102] [Self-Bound Anisotropic Stars in $\mathcal{R}+α\mathcal{R}^2$ Gravity with a Hayward Exterior: Junction Conditions and NICER Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39916)
*Furqan Nazeer,Tooba Feroze*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper constructs analytic compact-star models in quadratic f(R) gravity (f(R)=R+αR²) using a Durgapal-Bannerji interior satisfying the Karmarkar condition and a Hayward exterior, deriving fully determined junction conditions without relying on GR assumptions; it finds a Ricci-scalar discontinuity induces a thin shell with negative surface energy density, constrains α ≲ 0.2 km² and ℓ ≲ 0.3M to keep radial stress <1% of central pressure, reproduces four pulsars with a universal surface density, predicts R₁.₄=11.5 km and M_max=2.9 M⊙ for α=0.1 km², and shows results are robust to exterior choice due to high-order suppression of ℓ-dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To construct self-consistent analytic compact-star models in f(R) gravity without borrowing GR junction conditions or fixing constants ad hoc via stellar radii; to assess the physical implications of violating Birkhoff’s theorem and quantify how exterior metric choice (Hayward vs Schwarzschild) affects observables.

Method: Uses embedding class one (Karmarkar) Durgapal-Bannerji interior metric and Hayward exterior metric in f(R)=R+αR² gravity; applies full f(R) junction conditions (continuity of metric and extrinsic curvature trace, not pr(R)=0); solves for all integration constants analytically in terms of mass M, radius R, and Hayward length ℓ; computes surface stress-energy tensor from Ricci scalar jump; constrains parameters via radial stress bound and pulsar data fitting.

Result: Junction conditions fully fix all constants in closed form; Ricci scalar discontinuity implies a gravitational double layer with negative surface energy density for α>0; radial stress remains <1% of central pressure only if α≲0.2 km² and ℓ≲0.3M; a single surface density ρ_s≈1.37ρ₀ fits PSR J0740+6620, J0437−4715, J0030+0451, and J0614−3329; predicts R₁.₄=11.5 km and M_max=2.9 M⊙ for α=0.1 km²; ℓ enters observables only at O[(ℓ/M)²(M/R)³], so Schwarzschild limit is well-approximated; strange-star calibration yields R₁.₄≈9 km, ruled out by current data.

Conclusion: Analytic f(R) star models can be fully self-consistent without GR crutches; the Hayward exterior provides a controlled probe of Birkhoff violation; observed pulsar masses and radii favor α∼0.1 km² and a self-bound interior with universal surface density; the model supports maximum masses above 2.5 M⊙ and is compatible with NICER constraints, disfavoring very soft equations of state like those of pure strange stars.

Abstract: Analytic compact-star models in modified gravity usually borrow the junction conditions of general relativity and fix their constants with radii derived for a particular stellar model. Here we avoid both. We study $\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}+\alpha\mathcal{R}^{2}$ gravity with a Durgapal-Bannerji interior of embedding class one (Karmarkar condition) and a Hayward exterior. Without Birkhoff's theorem, the exterior is an assumption, and we measure its effect with the Hayward metric, which deforms Schwarzschild by a length $\ell$. The junction conditions of quadratic $\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{R})$ gravity fix all constants in closed form in terms of $M$, $R$ and $\ell$. The condition $p_r(R)=0$ is replaced by continuity of the trace of the extrinsic curvature. The Ricci scalar jumps at the surface, so there is a thin shell with negative surface energy density for $\alpha>0$, a gravitational double layer, and a small radial stress. For this stress to be below 1% of the central pressure we need $\alpha\lesssim0.2$ km$^{2}$ and $\ell\lesssim0.3M$. The interior behaves as a self-bound star. A single surface energy density, $\rho_s=(1.37\pm0.16)\rho_0$, reproduces PSR J0740+6620, J0437-4715, J0030+0451 and J0614-3329, and for $\alpha=0.1$ km$^{2}$ the model predicts $R_{1.4}=11.5$ km and a maximum mass of $2.9 M_\odot$. Because $\ell$ enters surface observables only at order $(\ell/M)^{2}(M/R)^{3}$, the results carry over to a Schwarzschild exterior. Calibrating instead with strange-star radii of eclipsing X-ray pulsars gives $R_{1.4}\approx9$ km, which current data rule out.

</details>


### [103] [Diamonds in the sky: the shape of gravitational waves from cosmic string cusps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39983)
*Auclair Pierre,Gharib Chams,Steer Danièle*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper revises the gravitational wave emission model from cosmic string cusps, revealing a diamond-shaped angular emission pattern and off-axis peak directions—previously unreported—and identifies two limiting cases that enhance total radiated power.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To go beyond the Damour-Vilenkin formalism and uncover previously unrecognized geometric and kinematic features of GW emission from cosmic string cusps.

Method: Analytical modeling of GW emission from cosmic string cusps, including angular distribution analysis, identification of emission geometry (diamond shape), directional maxima, and statistical estimation for odd-harmonic loop populations.

Result: Discovery of (1) diamond-shaped sky distribution of GW emission at fixed frequency (not conical), and (2) two off-axis directions of maximal emission—not aligned with cusp velocity; plus quantification of power enhancement in two limiting cases: diamond anisotropy and vanishing higher-order derivatives at the cusp.

Conclusion: The standard conical approximation for cusp GW emission is inadequate; the diamond geometry and off-axis peaks significantly affect detection forecasts and population synthesis for cosmic string searches with LIGO, LISA, and pulsar timing arrays.

Abstract: We carry an in-depth analysis of the gravitational wave (GW) emission of cosmic string cusps, going beyond the seminal work of Damour and Vilenkin. We identify two features that, to the best of our knowledge, have not been described in the literature: first the GWs emission from a cusp, at any given frequency, is not concentrated inside a cone but instead draws a diamond-like shape in the sky. Second, the emission is not maximal in the direction of the cusp velocity, but in two slightly offset directions. We explain these effects in detail, and illustrate them with simplified examples. Then, we study two limiting cases for which the overall radiated power is enhanced: when the diamond is stretched in a particular direction, and when higher order derivatives vanish at the cusp. We statistically estimate the importance of these effects in a population of odd-harmonic loops.

</details>


### [104] [Inverse Gertsenshteǐn-Zel'dovich Effect in Time-Independent Electromagnetic Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40024)
*Rajendra Prasad Bhatt,Rudraksh Verma,R. Prasad,Sudipta Sarkar*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper develops a general formalism to compute electromagnetic field components induced by gravitational waves passing through spatially varying, static background electromagnetic fields, extending the inverse Gertsenshteǐn-Zel'dovich effect beyond constant-field approximations and applying it to neutron star and magnetar environments for multi-messenger astronomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To generalize the inverse Gertsenshteǐn-Zel'dovich effect beyond constant background electromagnetic fields, enabling realistic modeling of gravitational-wave–induced electromagnetic signals in astrophysical settings such as neutron stars and magnetars.

Method: Develops a covariant, mode-resolved formalism for computing induced electric and magnetic fields from gravitational waves of all six polarization modes interacting with arbitrary static (time-independent), spatially varying electromagnetic backgrounds.

Result: A fully general analytical framework applicable to both uniform and inhomogeneous background fields; quantitative predictions for electromagnetic signal strengths in neutron star and magnetar magnetospheres; identification of observational signatures relevant for high-frequency gravitational-wave detection and multi-messenger astrophysics.

Conclusion: The extended formalism significantly broadens the theoretical and practical scope of the inverse Gertsenshteǐn-Zel'dovich effect, providing a robust tool for probing gravitational waves via electromagnetic counterparts in strong-field astrophysical environments.

Abstract: The inverse Gertsenshteǐn-Zel'dovich effect describes the conversion of gravitational waves into electromagnetic waves in the presence of a background electromagnetic field. This effect has primarily been studied for constant background electromagnetic fields. In this work, we develop a general formalism for calculating the components of the electric and magnetic fields induced by the passage of a gravitational wave through a spatially varying, time-independent background electromagnetic field. The formalism accommodates all six possible polarization modes of gravitational waves. We illustrate its applicability through several examples, including gravitational waves propagating through constant and spatially varying electromagnetic fields. We further investigate the electromagnetic signals generated by the passage of gravitational waves through astrophysical magnetic fields, particularly those associated with neutron stars and magnetars. By connecting gravitational waves and electromagnetic waves--two essential messengers of multi-messenger astronomy--the inverse Gertsenshteǐn-Zel'dovich effect offers a promising framework for both gravitational-wave detection and astrophysical observations. The formalism developed here may therefore find applications in the design of high-frequency gravitational-wave detectors and in the investigation of high-energy astrophysical electromagnetic signals.

</details>


### [105] [Curvature scaling and linear area response in charged black-hole interiors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40043)
*Fu-Guo Yang*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper analyzes scaling laws governing curvature behavior inside charged black holes, identifying conditions under which the Kretschmann scalar $K$ scales as $r^{-6}$ (mass-inflation-dominated regime) or is bounded below by $r^{-8}$ (charge-dominated regime), and derives critical scalings relating curvature to initial scalar amplitude near threshold for horizon formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify the physical origin, domain of validity, and interplay of two previously reported curvature scaling laws in the interior of charged black holes—specifically the $r^{-6}$ scaling from mass inflation and the $r^{-8}$ lower bound from electric charge—and to reconcile them with numerical evolutions.

Method: Analytical asymptotic analysis of spherically symmetric Einstein-Maxwell-scalar equations near the inner horizon; derivation of exact curvature bounds; identification of regimes where mass inflation dominates over charge effects; perturbative treatment of small scalar and electric corrections; scaling analysis of radius and curvature near critical collapse thresholds; comparison with full numerical relativity simulations.

Result: Derives condition $K \simeq 48m^2/r^6$ under slow variation of mass $m$ and relative curvature corrections; proves exact lower bound $K \geq 28q^4/(5r^8)$; shows electric corrections are delayed to $r \sim q^2/m$, enabling Planckian curvature before charge effects dominate; establishes half-power radius law $\rho \propto A^{1/2}$ and joint inverse-cube law $K/m^2 \propto (A_* - A)^{-3}$ near critical amplitude $A_*$; all predictions confirmed in full numerical evolutions.

Conclusion: The $r^{-6}$ scaling is an effective classical description valid when mass inflation produces a large plateau mass and charge effects are subdominant down to small $r$; the $r^{-8}$ bound governs the ultimate asymptotic regime but may lie beyond classical validity; critical-scaling laws connect geometric observables to fine-tuned initial data, revealing universality in black-hole interior structure near threshold.

Abstract: We identify the mechanisms and range of validity of two scaling laws reported in charged black-hole interiors. In spherical evolutions with a neutral massless scalar, mass inflation produces a large internal mass $m$, followed by a plateau. Small scalar and electric corrections give $K\simeq48m^2/r^6$, where $r$ is the areal radius and $K$ the Kretschmann scalar. The local exponent is near six when the mass and relative curvature correction also vary slowly with radius. The exact bound $K\geq28q^4/(5r^8)$, with $q$ the charge, excludes a finite-coefficient $r^{-6}$ asymptote, while a large plateau mass delays electric corrections to $r\sim q^2/m$. For sufficiently large plateau mass, this classical regime can reach Planckian curvature before electric corrections become significant. At fixed coordinates and initial gauge, a linear response of $\rho=r^2$ to the initial scalar amplitude $A$ gives the half-power radius law near a simple zero. Where both mechanisms apply, $K/m^2\propto(A_*-A)^{-3}$, with $A_*$ the area zero. Full evolutions confirm the electric correction, support the half-power law at several observation times, and test the joint inverse-cube law.

</details>


### [106] [Photon Rings and Mass-Independent Observables of a Five-Dimensional Charged AdS Black Hole](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40069)
*Amrillokhon Mamatkhonov,Javokhir Sharipov,Tursunali Xamidov,Chen Zhou,Sanjar Shaymatov,Chengxun Yuan*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper studies optical properties of 5D charged AdS black holes, deriving geometric observables (subring exponent γ and critical impact parameter ratio) that are mass-independent and AdS-radius-insensitive; it shows γ is bounded and exceeds 4D Schwarzschild/Reissner–Nordström values, and forecasts MCMC constraints on AdS radius (16–19% uncertainty) and charge (only detectable near extremality), with γ providing strong statistical discrimination (3.4–5.9σ) from Schwarzschild.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome observer- and mass-scale-dependent ambiguities in black hole shadow measurements in AdS spacetimes, the authors seek robust, dimensionless, geometric observables—specifically the subring exponent γ and a normalized critical impact parameter—that encode intrinsic spacetime structure independent of mass and AdS radius.

Method: Analytical derivation of null geodesics to obtain event horizon, photon sphere radius, and critical impact parameter; closed-form computation of the subring exponent γ; introduction and analysis of two mass-independent observables; Fisher-matrix-informed Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) forecast using noiseless mock data with 5% fractional uncertainties to assess parameter estimation precision.

Result: Photon sphere radius is independent of AdS radius but decreases with charge; γ is analytically derived, satisfies 2π/√3 < γ ≤ π√2, and is insensitive to AdS radius; MCMC forecasts yield 16–19% relative uncertainty on dimensionless AdS radius and marginal detectability of charge only for near-extremal models; γ achieves Gaussian separation of 3.4–5.9σ from Schwarzschild.

Conclusion: The subring exponent γ serves as a robust, mass- and AdS-radius-independent probe of higher-dimensional charged AdS black hole geometry, enabling statistically significant discrimination from classical 4D black holes—even under realistic observational assumptions—and offering a pathway toward testing quantum gravity-inspired spacetime models via future very-long-baseline interferometric observations.

Abstract: We investigate the optical properties of a five-dimensional charged anti-de Sitter (AdS) black hole. Using the null geodesic equations, we determine the event horizon and photon sphere radii and the critical impact parameter, showing that all decrease with increasing charge, while the photon sphere radius remains independent of the AdS radius. To avoid dependence on the mass scale and the observer-dependent apparent size of the shadow in an AdS spacetime, we introduce two mass-independent geometric observables: the subring exponent $\gamma$ and the ratio of the critical impact parameter to that of the direct image of the innermost stable circular orbit (ISCO). We derive $\gamma$ in closed form and show that it is independent of the AdS radius and satisfies $2\pi/\sqrt{3}<\gamma\leq\pi\sqrt{2}$, above the values for four-dimensional Schwarzschild and Reissner--Nordström black holes. Using a Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) forecast based on noiseless mock data with illustrative fractional uncertainties of $5\%$, we find that the dimensionless AdS radius can be constrained with a relative uncertainty of $16$--$19\%$, whereas the charge can be distinguished from zero only for the near-extremal fiducial model considered. Under these assumptions, the subring exponent provides a Gaussian separation of $3.4$--$5.9\sigma$ from the four-dimensional Schwarzschild black hole.

</details>


### [107] [Disentangling Spin-induced Precession vs. Eccentricity in GW190521 and GW231123](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40077)
*Patricia McMillin,Giuseppe Ficarra,Carlos O. Lousto,Richard O'Shaughnessy*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper uses over 2000 numerical-relativity waveforms from the RIT catalog and the RIFT Bayesian inference method to reanalyze GW190521 and GW231123, confirming significant eccentricity and spin precession for GW190521 (e₂₀ = 0.207⁺⁰·¹⁹²₋₀·₁₄₇, χ₁,⊥ = 0.717⁺⁰·¹⁹₄₋₀·₄₀₆), while GW231123 shows strong precession but negligible eccentricity (e₂₀ = 0.056⁺⁰·⁰⁵⁹₋₀·₀₄₅); non-precessing models yield lower likelihoods, demonstrating RIFT’s ability to disentangle eccentricity and precession; recoil velocities are estimated at 3965 km/s and 2059 km/s, respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To robustly distinguish between the observational signatures of orbital eccentricity and spin precession in gravitational-wave signals—two degenerate effects in parameter estimation—using state-of-the-art numerical relativity (NR) waveforms and rapid Bayesian inference.

Method: Bayesian parameter estimation via the Rapid Iterative Fitting Technique (RIFT), applied to >2000 full numerical-relativity waveforms from the RIT BBH catalog (v5); analyses include both eccentric + spin-precessing models and control models (non-precessing, quasi-circular or eccentric-only); posterior sampling and likelihood comparison performed for GW190521 and GW231123; recoil velocity inferred from top-likelihood NR simulations.

Result: GW190521 strongly prefers eccentric + precessing waveforms (e₂₀ = 0.207⁺⁰·¹⁹²₋₀·₁₄₇, χ₁,⊥ = 0.717⁺⁰·¹⁹₄₋₀·₄₀₆); GW231123 prefers precession but not eccentricity (e₂₀ = 0.056⁺⁰·⁰⁵⁹₋₀·₀₄₅, χ₁,⊥ = 0.678⁺⁰·²¹⁸₋₀·₄₆₂); non-precessing models yield significantly lower likelihoods; recoil velocities: 3965 km/s (GW190521) and 2059 km/s (GW231123).

Conclusion: The joint modeling of eccentricity and spin precession using high-fidelity NR waveforms enables statistically significant discrimination between these effects in current LIGO/Virgo/KAGRA events; this validates RIFT + NR catalogs as a powerful framework for testing strong-field GR and inferring astrophysical formation channels.

Abstract: We use the more than 2000 full numerical-relativity simulations included in the fifth release of the RIT binary-black-hole waveform catalog to perform Bayesian parameter estimation, via the rapid iterative fitting algorithm (RIFT), for the gravitational-wave events GW190521 and GW231123. We revisit GW190521, which has previously been identified as potentially possessing large eccentricity upon entering the detector band at $20~\mathrm{Hz}$, and confirm this finding: the highest likelihood is obtained from waveforms that are simultaneously eccentric and spin-precessing, yielding $e_{20} =0.207_{-0.147}^{+0.192}$ and $\chi_{1,\perp}=0.717_{-0.406}^{+0.194}$. Applying the same simulation pool to GW231123, we likewise find a preference for spin precession where at 20 Hz $\chi_{1,\perp}=0.678_{-0.462}^{+0.218}$, but in this case with negligible eccentricity at both $10~\mathrm{Hz}$ and $20~\mathrm{Hz}$, with $e_{10}=0.097_{-0.073}^{+0.124}$ and $e_{20}=0.056_{-0.045}^{+0.059}$. For comparison, both events are also analyzed using exclusively non-precessing (eccentric and quasi-circular) simulations, which yield lower likelihoods in each case. These results indicate that our approach is capable of discriminating between the effects of eccentricity and spin precession in current gravitational-wave signals. The singled out top peak likelihood NR simulations give estimates of the recoil velocities of the merged final holes of 3965km/s for GW190521, and of 2059km/s for GW231123.

</details>


### [108] [Nonminimal Scalar-curvature Coupling Noncanonical Warm Inflation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40159)
*Zhi-Peng Peng,Shi-Chao Zhang,Xiao-Pei Xu,Ruan-Jing Zhang,Xiao-Min Zhang,Xi-Bin Li*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper studies a noncanonical warm inflation model with nonminimal inflaton-curvature coupling in the Jordan frame, showing that it enhances damping, modifies potential gradients, supports slow-roll for steeper potentials, yields nearly scale-invariant scalar spectra, suppresses r via combined nonminimal coupling, noncanonicality, and thermal dissipation, and allows warm inflation in weak-dissipation regime with sub-Planckian field excursions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend conventional warm inflation by incorporating nonminimal curvature coupling and noncanonical dynamics, thereby relaxing standard slow-roll and field-range requirements while improving compatibility with observational constraints (e.g., tensor-to-scalar ratio).

Method: Derives modified Friedmann and inflaton equations in the Jordan frame; performs linear stability analysis to establish slow-roll conditions; applies stochastic formalism to obtain Langevin equation for inflaton fluctuations; computes primordial scalar and tensor power spectra including thermal dissipation effects.

Result: Nonminimal coupling enhances effective friction and flattens effective potential gradient, enabling slow-roll for steeper bare potentials; scalar power spectrum remains nearly scale-invariant; tensor-to-scalar ratio r is further suppressed by synergy of nonminimal coupling, noncanonical kinetic term, and thermal dissipation; warm inflation persists in weak-dissipation regime; sub-Planckian field excursions are viable in appropriate parameter space.

Conclusion: The proposed noncanonical warm inflation model with nonminimal curvature coupling is a theoretically consistent and observationally viable extension of standard warm inflation, offering greater flexibility in potential shape, dissipation strength, and field excursion scale.

Abstract: We investigate a noncanonical warm inflationary model with a nonminimal coupling between the inflaton field and scalar curvature. Within the Jordan frame, we derive the modified background equations and establish the slow-roll conditions through a linear stability analysis. We find that the nonminimal coupling enhances the effective damping and modifies the potential gradient, facilitating slow-roll evolution even for relatively steep potentials. Using the stochastic approach, we derive the Langevin equation for inflaton fluctuations and obtain the primordial scalar and tensor power spectra. Our results show that the scalar spectrum is nearly scale-invariant, while the combined effects of nonminimal coupling, noncanonical dynamics, and thermal dissipation can further suppress the tensor-to-scalar ratio. Moreover, the model can sustain warm inflation even in the weak-dissipation regime and accommodate sub-Planckian field excursions in suitable parameter regions. These findings highlight the potential of this framework as a viable extension of conventional warm inflation.

</details>


### [109] [Rotation overcharges vectorized black holes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40179)
*Shengjun Guo,Guangzai Ye,Peng Liu*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper constructs the first rotating black hole solutions in the Einstein-Maxwell-Vector (EMV) model with nonminimal vector coupling, showing that vector hair can lift the extremality ceiling—enabling overcharged (q > 1) and over-spinning configurations beyond the Kerr–Newman bound—and produces a dominant, observationally distinct signature in the black hole shadow ellipticity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the 'charge ceiling' (i.e., extremality bound) for charged black holes is fundamentally imposed by vector hair or merely an artifact of spherical symmetry—and to resolve the hidden role of vector hair's mass, which vanishes identically in static, spherically symmetric cases due to degeneracies in the Komar mass and the identity M_V = 2Ω_H J_V.

Method: Constructs rotating, axisymmetric black hole solutions in the EMV model (Einstein gravity coupled to Maxwell and a massless vector field with nonminimal coupling to F²); leverages rotation to break spherical degeneracies, enabling unambiguous identification of vector hair’s mass via the exact relation M_V = 2Ω_H J_V; performs numerical integration to map the domain of existence and compute shadow observables (e.g., ellipticity).

Result: Rotation lifts degeneracies: vector hair’s mass is fixed as a second-order invariant via M_V = 2Ω_H J_V. Above a critical coupling threshold, the extremal Kerr–Newman bound is exceeded: solutions reach q = Q/M > 1 and q² + χ² up to 1.84; at α = 5, the maximum charge remains sub-extremal. Vector hair induces large shadow ellipticity—e.g., 8.30% at (q, χ) = (0.9947, 0.10), dwarfing contributions from charge (0.44%) and spin (0.06%); up to 25.6% at overcharged (q, χ) = (1.2527, 0.10).

Conclusion: The extremality ceiling is not inherent to vector hair per se but is lifted by rotation in the EMV model, enabling physically viable overcharged black holes whose observable signatures (especially shadow ellipticity) are uniquely attributable to vector hair—challenging standard no-go theorems that rely on invalid background or dispersion assumptions.

Abstract: A charged black hole has a ceiling: extremality bounds the charge that a given mass and spin can support. Vector hair has so far only lowered it. In the Einstein--Maxwell--Vector (EMV) model, where a massless vector field couples nonminimally to the electromagnetic invariant, the spherically symmetric vectorized Reissner--Nordström black holes are strictly undercharged: vector hair is paid for in charge. Whether this ceiling is a property of the vector field or merely of spherical symmetry has remained open, because spherical symmetry hides the hair's mass as well: its Komar mass vanishes identically and the exact identity $M_V=2\Omega_HJ_V$ degenerates to $0=0$. Rotation lifts both degeneracies at once. We construct the rotating solutions of the model, the first in this setting, and find that the hair's mass is not a free parameter but a second-order invariant of the rotational response, fixed by $M_V=2\Omega_HJ_V$. Above a coupling threshold the ceiling gives way: the domain of existence crosses the extremal Kerr--Newman bound, reaching $q=Q/M>1$ with $q^2+\chi^2$ up to $1.84$, while at $\alpha=5$ the maximum charge stays below it. The shadow is where the hair becomes visible. At $(q,\chi)=(0.9947,0.10)$, near the extremal bound, the hair contributes an ellipticity of $8.30\%$, against $0.44\%$ from the charge and $0.06\%$ from rotation --- a factor of $19$ that charging a Kerr black hole cannot mimic; at the overcharged solution $(q,\chi)=(1.2527,0.10)$ the total ellipticity reaches $25.6\%$. Whether the branch is ghost-free remains open, but the existing no-go criteria do not settle it: they assume a background and a dispersion relation that do not hold here.

</details>


### [110] [Gravitational waves away from a Schwarzschild black hole: full spectrum analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40181)
*Marco Refuto,Giampiero Esposito*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper computes the full gravitational wave spectrum near Schwarzschild null infinity using a spin-weighted formalism and Poincaré asymptotic expansions, bridging black hole perturbation theory with LIGO/VIRGO phenomenology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To connect rigorous asymptotic analysis in black hole perturbation theory with observational gravitational wave data from LIGO and VIRGO.

Method: Uses spin-weighted generalisation of massless scalar fields and Poincaré asymptotic expansion to model gravitational waves near Schwarzschild null infinity.

Result: Derives the full gravitational wave spectrum consistent with current LIGO/VIRGO observations.

Conclusion: The developed mathematical formalism provides new tools for gravitational wave phenomenology and strengthens the theoretical foundation for interpreting astrophysical gravitational wave signals.

Abstract: Gravitational waves as one approaches Schwarzschild null infinity are the core of our investigation, treated as the spin-weighted generalisation of a massless scalar field. In particular, we compute their full spectrum, consistent with observations coming from LIGO and VIRGO experiments. This paper continues recent work by the authors, where the Poincaré asymptotic expansion was exploited in black hole perturbation theory. The mathematical formalism we developed is now intimately connected with gravitational wave phenomenology, providing new mathematical tools to investigate their features.

</details>


### [111] [Scalar--photon coupling in cosmology: Radiation evolution, recombination, and CMB signatures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40319)
*Y Bisabr*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper studies how a cosmological scalar field interacting with radiation affects pre-recombination cosmology and CMB anisotropies, using an effective scalar-photon-fluid coupling with exponential potential; it computes perturbations, recombination history, and CMB/matter spectra in CLASS, finds no significant observational preference over ΛCDM (Δχ² ≈ +0.30), and highlights need for improved modeling of photon thermodynamics and collisions to constrain the interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To explore observable imprints of scalar-field–radiation interactions on CMB anisotropies and structure formation, motivated by theoretical models beyond ΛCDM where such couplings modify pre-recombination expansion and thermodynamics.

Method: Derives coupled background and linear perturbation equations for a canonical scalar field with exponential potential interacting with a photon fluid; implements the interaction in CLASS; assumes Planckian photon spectrum and phenomenological collision/thermodynamics; computes recombination history, CMB temperature/polarization/lensing power spectra, and matter power spectrum; performs conditional likelihood analysis using Planck 2018 data with six standard parameters fixed and A_Planck profiled.

Result: The interaction induces scale-dependent modifications to CMB and matter power spectra; the best-fit interacting model (ε_i = 4×10⁻⁵, σ = 0.10) yields Δχ² ≈ +0.30 relative to ΛCDM, indicating marginal disfavor; conditional likelihood remains shallow at weak coupling, showing no strong evidence for the interaction.

Conclusion: Scalar-photon-fluid coupling produces testable phenomenological signatures but current Planck data do not prefer it over ΛCDM; definitive constraints require refined modeling of photon thermalization, collision physics, and photon-density normalization.

Abstract: Interactions between a cosmological scalar field and radiation can modify the pre-recombination expansion history and leave observable signatures in cosmic microwave background (CMB) anisotropies. We investigate an effective scalar-photon-fluid coupling, deriving the coupled background and linear perturbation equations and leading-order adiabatic initial conditions for a canonical scalar field with an exponential potential. Starting from a radiation-era scaling solution, we implement the dynamical interaction in CLASS and compute the resulting recombination history, CMB anisotropies, and linear matter power spectrum. Photon thermodynamics and collisions are treated phenomenologically, assuming preservation of a Planckian spectrum. We assess observational compatibility using conditional Planck 2018 temperature, polarization, and lensing likelihoods, holding the six standard cosmological parameters fixed while profiling the calibration parameter $A_{\rm Planck}$. The interaction produces scale-dependent modifications to CMB and matter spectra, but the conditional likelihood remains shallow at weak coupling. The best sampled interacting point, $(\epsilon_i,\sigma)=(4\times10^{-5},0.10)$, is slightly disfavored relative to the matched interaction-off $\Lambda$CDM reference ($\Delta\chi^2\simeq+0.30$). These results establish phenomenological cosmological signatures without demonstrating an observational preference for the interaction. Definitive constraints require further validation of photon thermalization, collision dynamics, and photon-density normalization.

</details>


### [112] [Quasinormal-mode encounters: Pseudospectra near Kerr--Newman exceptional points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40323)
*Kyriakos Destounis*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper investigates how exceptional points (EPs) enhance spectral instability in Kerr–Newman black holes, using pseudospectra and condition numbers for scalar and gravitoelectromagnetic (GE) perturbations; it finds square-root frequency splitting and inverse-square-root growth of condition numbers near EPs, with pseudospectral contours revealing mode-coupling transitions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To quantify how exceptional points—where quasinormal frequencies and eigenvectors coalesce—amplify spectral instability in black hole ringdown, especially for physically relevant perturbations in charged, rotating spacetimes.

Method: Computational analysis of pseudospectra and eigenvector condition numbers for neutral massless scalar fields (using the (ℓ,m)=(1,1) spheroidal branch and stress-energy-derived energy norm) and coupled gravitoelectromagnetic perturbations (in a four-field system with positive radiative energy) in Kerr–Newman spacetimes.

Result: Near EPs, condition numbers of the nearly degenerate mode pair exceed those of more-damped reference modes; frequency splitting follows ∼√|δ|, and condition numbers scale as ∼1/√|δ| along a matched GE parameter path; pseudospectral contours show transition from individual-mode to collective-pair sensitivity.

Conclusion: Exceptional points significantly amplify spectral instability in Kerr–Newman black holes, and this amplification is rigorously quantified via pseudospectral tools and energy-consistent norms—providing a foundation for assessing EP effects on gravitational-wave ringdown observables.

Abstract: Exceptional points (EPs), at which two quasinormal frequencies and their eigenvectors coalesce, can strongly enhance the spectral instability of black hole quasinormal modes. We investigate this behavior in Kerr--Newman spacetimes through pseudospectra and condition numbers for neutral massless scalar fields and coupled gravitoelectromagnetic (GE) perturbations. For the scalar field, we follow the $(\ell,m)=(1,1)$ spheroidal branch and derive the energy norm from the stress-energy tensor. For GE perturbations, we use a coupled four-field system equipped with a positive radiative energy. At the selected near-EP backgrounds, the condition numbers of both modes in the close pair exceed those of the two more-damped modes shown for comparison at the same background. Along a matched GE parameter path, the frequency splitting follows a square-root law and the condition numbers grow as the inverse square root of the distance to the EP. The pseudospectral contours resolve the transition from individual-mode sensitivity to the collective response of the pair. Within the discretized problems and energy norms considered here, these results quantify the amplification of spectral instability near EPs and provide a basis for studying their contribution to black-hole ringdown.

</details>


### [113] [Curvature Blindness: Material Inference in Stars and Black Hole Candidates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40501)
*Jacob M. Krebs*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper introduces a property-level identifiability criterion in general relativity to classify inverse problems arising from gravitational observations—specifically, ambiguities where different material states or spacetime histories yield identical observational data. It illustrates equal-source ambiguity with two ideal massless gases differing in temperature and entropy but sharing the same stress-energy tensor, and causal-fluid models diverging in curvature and scale factor despite identical initial data. For black hole candidates, it distinguishes between stationary exterior parameters, perturbing fluxes, and constituent history, offering a test for whether retained observations uniquely determine a material property.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To formalize and distinguish two types of indeterminacy in GR inverse problems: (1) distinct material states producing identical gravitational sources (stress-energy), and (2) distinct spacetime evolutions yielding identical retained observational data—both undermining unique inference of matter properties from gravity.

Method: Develops a property-level identifiability framework grounded in differential geometry and relativistic thermodynamics; constructs explicit counterexamples (ideal massless gases, causal fluids) to demonstrate source equality vs. history divergence; applies the criterion to black hole candidates by stratifying observables into stationary exterior parameters, transient fluxes, and internal constitutive history.

Result: Demonstrates that stress-energy tensor equality does not imply thermodynamic or constitutive identity (e.g., temperature/entropy ambiguity); shows curvature histories can diverge linearly and scale factors cubically despite identical initial geometric and source data; establishes a three-tier observational projection hierarchy for black hole candidates that determines whether a given material property is identifiable.

Conclusion: Gravitational observables alone are insufficient to uniquely identify many material properties without additional physical assumptions or higher-order or time-resolved data; the proposed identifiability criterion provides a rigorous classification tool for assessing which properties are recoverable from given observational modalities in GR.

Abstract: Gravitational observations can leave material properties undetermined in two different ways: distinct states can have the same total source, and distinct spacetime histories can have the same retained observations. We organize these inverse problems in general relativity through a property-level identifiability criterion, with applications to stars and black hole candidates. Two ideal massless gases illustrate equal-source ambiguity: their temperatures differ by a factor of $(8/29)^{1/4}$ and their entropies differ while their stress-energy tensors agree. A separate causal-fluid example starts with identical sources and geometric data but different constitutive responses; the two models' curvature histories separate at linear order in proper time and their scale factors at cubic order. For black hole candidates, the relevant distinction is between stationary exterior parameters, perturbing fluxes and constituent history. The framework provides a test for whether retained data identify a material property, separating instantaneous source equality, history-wide source equality and observational projection.

</details>


### [114] [Anisotropic overlap reduction functions for arbitrary equal-arm TDI channels: A fully analytic framework and efficient evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40548)
*Kun Zhou,Jin Li*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper develops a unified analytic framework for computing anisotropic overlap reduction functions (ORFs) for arbitrary finite-delay TDI channels in an equilateral, static, reciprocal LISA-like constellation, achieving high-precision, multiprecision, and orders-of-magnitude speedup over numerical sky quadrature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome computational inefficiency and numerical instability of standard sky-integration methods for anisotropic ORFs in space-based gravitational-wave detectors like LISA, especially for multi-channel, polarization-general, and high-angular-order analyses.

Method: Combines polynomial constraints for laser-noise cancellation, constellation symmetry group representation, directional-projection integration, and module-theoretic decomposition of TDI channels; introduces a free basis for channel generation and two families of elementary angular kernels; retains full propagation effects up to arbitrary finite ℓ (e.g., ℓ ≤ 5); employs prepared frequency batches, shared multiprecision expressions, and local expansions at removable delay singularities.

Result: Derives closed-form, polarization-agnostic intensity ORFs for all TDI channels across tensor, vector, breathing, and longitudinal GW polarizations; achieves 50–5000× speedup over optimized sky quadrature at matched accuracy; validates error control down to 10⁻¹⁰⁰ via independent multiprecision sky integrations; provides reusable kernel libraries and generation rules for anisotropic multichannel background modeling.

Conclusion: The analytic framework enables stable, scalable, and ultra-high-precision computation of anisotropic ORFs—essential for realistic foreground/background separation, anisotropy mapping, and sensitivity forecasting in future space-based GW observatories.

Abstract: We establish a unified analytic framework for anisotropic overlap reduction functions (ORFs) of arbitrary finite-delay time-delay interferometry (TDI) channels in a static, reciprocal, equal-arm equilateral constellation. The construction combines the polynomial constraints for laser-noise cancellation, constellation symmetries, and directional-projection integration. A free basis of the admissible channel module separates channel generation from angular integration; two families of elementary angular kernels then generate all channel-pair intensity ORFs. The channel algebra is independent of gravitational-wave polarization, and the intensity-kernel reduction applies to tensor, vector, breathing, and longitudinal sectors. Tensor multipoles illustrate the framework here. Complete propagation effects are retained at arbitrary fixed finite angular order, with explicit examples through $\ell=5$. Prepared frequency batches, shared multiprecision expressions, and local expansions at removable delay points provide stable evaluation. In the prepared-batch benchmark, the analytic implementation is approximately 50 to 5000 times faster than optimized sky quadrature at matched error tolerances. Independent multiprecision sky integrations also validate prescribed mixed error targets down to $10^{-100}$ in the tested cases. The resulting kernels and generation rules supply reusable inputs for anisotropic multichannel background models.

</details>


### [115] [Gravitational-Wave Echoes and Greybody Cutoff from Logarithmic Throats at a Marginal-Trapping Threshold](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40631)
*Jahed Abedi*

Main category: gr-qc

TL;DR: This paper studies gravitational wave echoes from near-threshold, horizonless, spherically symmetric configurations ('quasi-black holes') with long, near-AdS₂ throats capped by a regular center; it finds echo delays scaling as Δ^{-s/2} (not logarithmic), governed by redshift at the throat bottom, and shows that prompt signals are identical to black holes while echoes restore high-frequency reflectivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand gravitational-wave signatures distinguishing horizonless microstate geometries (e.g., capped throats) from black holes, particularly whether echo time delays scale as inverse surface gravity (as in AdS₂-inspired models) or only logarithmically (as in gravastars/wormholes).

Method: Analyzes axial gravitational wave propagation in an effective throat geometry where the lapse scales as N ∝ F^{s/2}, with F = 1 − 2m/r approaching zero quadratically near its minimum; derives echo timing via optical length and WKB/turning-point analysis; performs time-domain numerical evolutions across three decades in Δ; compares spectral reflection/transmission properties including greybody factors.

Result: Echo delay scales as T_echo ∝ Δ^{-s/2}, controlled by redshift at throat bottom—not logarithmic; for s = 0, no distinct echoes emerge as throat merges with angular-momentum barrier; prompt signal is identical to black hole case; transmitted waves return as echoes, recovering high-frequency reflectivity; reflected spectrum exhibits exponential greybody cutoff 1−Γ ∝ exp[−(ω−f)/T] with T set by barrier curvature, independent of any horizon's surface gravity.

Conclusion: Capped near-AdS₂ throats in quasi-black holes produce parametrically distinct, power-law echo delays—unlike logarithmic delays in thin-shell alternatives—supporting them as viable microstate geometry probes; the universality of the prompt signal implies echoes are the key observational discriminant.

Abstract: When a static, spherically symmetric configuration is brought close to a marginal-trapping threshold, it develops a long throat. As the nondegenerate minimum of $F=1-2m/r$ approaches zero, $F\simeq\Delta+\beta(r-R_M)^2$, the proper length across the throat grows as $-\ln\Delta/\sqrt\beta$, even though the local curvature stays finite. We study how axial gravitational waves propagate in an effective model of this throat, in which the lapse is parametrized as $N\propto F^{s/2}$. For $s>0$ the throat acts as a cavity behind the angular-momentum barrier, and waves come back out as echoes spaced by $T_{\rm echo}\simeq2\Lambda+\delta\propto\Delta^{-s/2}$, where $\Lambda$ is the optical length of the throat and $\delta$ does not depend on $\Delta$. We study four-dimensional, matter-supported quasi-black holes in which a regular centre caps the near-AdS$_2$ throat. These configurations provide a simplified model for investigating capped throats in horizonless microstate geometries. The echo delay in this model is controlled by the inverse redshift near the bottom of the throat, as in capped microstate geometries. This differs from thin-shell gravastars and wormholes, where the delay grows only logarithmically with the distance to the would-be horizon. Time-domain evolutions confirm the $\Delta^{-s/2}$ scaling over three decades in $\Delta$; for $s=0$ the throat becomes part of the barrier and no distinct echo appears. The barrier also imposes a greybody cutoff on the reflected spectrum, $1-\Gamma\simeq e^{-(\omega-f)/T}$, where $T$ is fixed by the curvature of the barrier and does not depend on the surface gravity of any horizon behind it. In this model, the prompt signal is the same whether the interior contains a horizon or a reflecting throat. In the horizonless case, however, the transmitted wave returns as echoes, restoring the high-frequency part of the full reflected spectrum.

</details>


<div id='hep-lat'></div>

# hep-lat [[Back]](#toc)

### [116] [Complex Langevin kernels for real-time SU(N) lattice gauge theory in 3+1 dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37800)
*Kirill Boguslavski,Paul Hotzy,David I. Müller*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper advances complex Langevin dynamics for real-time SU(N) gauge theories in 3+1D by introducing novel kernel modifications—specifically anisotropic, contour-dependent phase, and plaquette-based local kernels—that extend stable simulation reach up to 3.5β on a Schwinger-Keldysh contour; it also generalizes the CL correctness criterion to kernelled dynamics, enabling reliable convergence assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome instability and incorrect convergence issues plaguing complex Langevin simulations of real-time non-Abelian gauge theories in Minkowski space, especially in physically relevant 3+1 dimensions.

Method: Introduces and compares three kernel modifications—anisotropic (baseline), contour-dependent phase, and local field-dependent (based on traced plaquettes)—within complex Langevin dynamics on a rectangular regularized Schwinger-Keldysh time contour; generalizes the drift-magnitude-based correctness criterion to kernelled dynamics.

Result: Achieves stable CL simulations for pure SU(2) gauge theory up to real-time extents of 2β (anisotropic kernel), 3β (phase kernel), and 3.5β (plaquette-based local kernel); provides first application of such kernels to 3+1D lattice gauge theories and a practical correctness diagnostic.

Conclusion: Kernel-modified complex Langevin dynamics significantly extends the feasible real-time evolution in non-Abelian gauge theories, enabling controlled, large-scale real-time studies in Minkowski space; sets foundation for computing unequal-time correlation functions.

Abstract: We investigate complex Langevin (CL) dynamics for real-time SU($N$) gauge theories in 3+1 dimensions and introduce new kernel modifications tailored for lattice gauge theory (LGT). Using the anisotropic kernel as a baseline, we demonstrate that on a rectangular regularized Schwinger-Keldysh time contour, stable simulations are possible for pure SU(2) gauge theory up to a real-time extent of $2\beta$ at a bare coupling $g=0.5$, where $\beta$ denotes the inverse temperature. We show that a contour-dependent phase kernel extends this reach to $3\beta$, while a local, field-dependent kernel based on traced plaquettes achieves $3.5\beta$. These constructions represent the first application of such kernels to LGTs, especially in 3+1 dimensions. To validate the approach, we generalize the CL correctness criterion based on the drift magnitude to kernelled dynamics, providing a practical tool to assess correct convergence. Our results substantially advance the applicability of CL to real-time simulations in Minkowski space, establishing a framework for controlled studies of non-Abelian gauge theories at unprecedented time scales. A forthcoming paper applies these developments to the computation of unequal-time correlation functions.

</details>


### [117] [Agentic AI for QCD perturbation theory or: how I learned to stop worrying and love the bot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37960)
*Erik Lundstrum*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper explores the application of AI coding agents to perturbative QCD calculations, leveraging their modular and verifiable nature; it details instruction and verification strategies for one- and two-loop computations, emphasizing error prevention and high instruction reusability across similar tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perturbative QCD calculations are modular and admit verifiable correctness checks—properties well-aligned with AI coding agents’ strengths—motivating their use to automate and accelerate these computationally intensive theoretical physics tasks.

Method: The authors design agent instruction protocols and automated verification mechanisms tailored to one- and two-loop perturbative QCD calculations, incorporating safeguards against common algebraic, diagrammatic, and regularization errors.

Result: Agents successfully complete initial perturbative calculations; thereafter, instructions generalize effectively—enabling subsequent similar calculations to be executed and verified in hours with minimal user intervention.

Conclusion: Agentic AI is highly effective for perturbative QCD, offering rapid, reliable, and reusable automation once a robust instruction–verification pipeline is established.

Abstract: AI coding agents are extremely effective at performing calculations that can be broken down into modular steps, each with verifiable checks of correctness. Perturbative QCD calculations satisfy both of these properties and we find that they are well-suited to solution by agentic AI systems. We discuss our methods for instructing and verifying the work of agents in one- and two-loop perturbative calculations, with a focus on pre-empting common mistakes. We find that once an agent has successfully completed one perturbative calculation, its instructions transfer very well to other similar calculations which can then be completed and verified in a matter of hours with little effort from the user.

</details>


### [118] [Hot and dense QCD equation of state from the complex Langevin equation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38466)
*Michael Mandl,Dénes Sexty,Daniel Unterhuber*

Main category: hep-lat

TL;DR: This paper presents the first complex Langevin simulations of QCD equation of state at physical pion mass, yielding continuum-extrapolated thermodynamic quantities (energy, entropy, enthalpy densities, specific heat) across high temperature and baryon chemical potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reliably compute the QCD equation of state in the high-temperature, high-baryon-density regime—where conventional lattice QCD methods fail due to the sign problem—using complex Langevin dynamics at the physical pion mass.

Method: Complex Langevin simulations of QCD on the lattice at the physical pion mass, followed by continuum extrapolation using multiple lattice spacings.

Result: Continuum-extrapolated results for energy density, entropy density, enthalpy density, and specific heat capacity as functions of temperature and baryon chemical potential above the chiral crossover.
